Transceiver light-source reliability test equipment order momentum LIKELY MORE THAN 3X IN 2026E, with growth remaining strong in 2027** (JPM · Jerry Tsai, OW, PT NT$3,000 → NT$3,160, 2026-07-30). ⚠️ Chroma's positioning argument: decades of optical-test experience "from iPhone 3D sensing to optical module/CPO" let it **co-develop or even DEFINE test solutions.**

Events: JPM COHR + LITE analyst callback 2026-08-14 10am ET; JPM HW & Semis Management Access Forum 08-12 with AAOI, CDNS, LITE, LRCX plus optical panels (Ranovus, Avicena) and the 650 Group.**

## What it is / why it matters
AI interconnect is the bandwidth plumbing that decides how big a model you can train and how efficiently you can serve it — and it is in the middle of a copper-to-optics phase change. The mechanism is simple physics: every time you double interconnect bandwidth, the reach a high-fidelity electrical signal can travel halves, so as scale-up fabrics get both faster (800G → 1.6T per port) and physically larger (e.g. Rubin Ultra NVL576 = eight NVL72s in one fabric), copper runs out of room and the signal has to go photonic (Mike/Rothschild-Redburn, 2026-05-19). That pulls optics from a niche outside-the-box product into the heart of the rack, opening an *incremental* TAM that these vendors' products were never inside before — which is why a basket that "used to trade in the teens" now trades 25–30x CY28 (Meta Marshall/MS, 2026-05-07). The buy-side reason to care: the spend is being levered three ways at once — scale-out (between racks), scale-up (inside the rack), and scale-across (between data centers/buildings) — and the binding constraint through 2028 is not demand but **laser/InP supply** plus manufacturing ramp. This is a picks-and-shovels, supply-gated up-cycle, and the cleanest expression is the light source, not the module.

Speaker attribution is hard.)_
  - **⚔️ THE CPO-CLOCK DISAGREEMENT, and it splits by who sells what.** **Hurlston (LITE): "no delay in CPO; NPO being brought forward"** — CPO production on track with an *increased* demand signal. **Murray (AAOI): "there's a disconnect between the speed at which investors think things happen and what actually needs to happen to bring CPO to market at scale… end-2027 they expect to start and see some orders, but it's a 2028 story."** ⚠️ **How to hold both:** LITE sells the **light source into NVDA's CPO engine** and is seeing *order/prepay* behaviour now (it forced the AXT substrate reservation — see below); AAOI sells **modules** and is describing *volume revenue* timing. Order signal ≠ revenue ramp. Net: this page's standing "**partial 2028, meaningful 2029**" consensus (650 Group, Jefferies expert, SemiAnalysis, Redburn, UBS) is **untouched on the revenue clock**, while the **procurement clock has clearly pulled forward** — which is precisely the pattern that shows up as prepays and capacity reservations before it shows up in P&Ls.
  - **🔴 THE RECONCILIATION THAT MATTERS: "China localized CW" is true only BELOW the tier that pays.** The Jefferies expert call above scored Chinese **CW-laser localization as "done"** (Yuanjie displacing Sumitomo into Innolight/Eoptolink/COHR). Hurlston, from inside the incumbent: **"haven't seen a lot of lasers coming out of China,"** LITE's target band is **70–100mW**, he **"doesn't see Chinese at 100mW,"** and there is **"a big gap for NPO/CPO from 100mW."** ⚠️ **Both are right at different power tiers — commodity/low-power CW is localized; the ≥100mW NPO/CPO band is not.** That band is where the ASP and the moat sit, so **the China-substitution scorecard on this page should be read power-tier-by-power-tier, not component-by-component.** Corollary from AAOI: **"high-power narrow-linewidth laser is critical for ELS, so those who have the former can make the latter"** — the high-power tier is the gate to the ELS attach, not just to the laser sale.
  - **NPO is bigger than CPO on lane count — the sharpest sizing claim of the week.** Hurlston: **~6 meaningful NPO engagements**, and **the lane intensity is HIGHER than NVDA's CPO** ("# of NPO optical lanes from north to south is considerably higher — more opportunity, more customers"). Mechanism: NPO is the **"Tesla 3 vs Model X"** — most of the performance, cheaper — but **"eventually everyone will have to go to CPO,"** and NPO builds the competence that carries players into CPO. ✓ Consistent with this page's "NPO is the interim step" thread (650 Group/Weckel, UBS, TSEM) but **inverts the implied hierarchy**: on lanes, the interim step may be the larger near-term pool. AAOI corroborates NPO durability from the other side (an **NPO pioneer with MSFT in 2017/18**, **no change in NPO demand**), while still calling **CPO the eventual major player**.

It is not happening.

- **NEW (2026-07-29) — 🔴 LUMENTUM'S IR PUTS A DATE ON COPPER'S WALL — "LATE 2027" — AND IN THE SAME MEETING HANDS THE BEARS THEIR BEST NUMBER: JAPANESE EML CAPACITY UP EIGHT-FOLD IN TWO AND A HALF YEARS. THE MOST DIRECT MANAGEMENT EVIDENCE THIS PAGE HAS ON EITHER SIDE OF THE CPO-TIMING DEBATE (Aurelion Research / PhotonCap "A Conversation with Lumentum", 2026-07-29).** ⚠️ **Provenance first, because it governs how much weight each claim carries: this is a JOINT INDEPENDENT publication, not a bank. The management meeting and investment analysis were prepared by Aurelion Research (who met Kathryn Ta, VP of Investor Relations); the technical comments are PhotonCap's. The authors state that interview responses are paraphrased from their notes and "do not represent verbatim statements by Lumentum or its management." Management, Aurelion's analysis and PhotonCap's commentary are kept separate below.**
  - **THE TIMING LEG — MANAGEMENT, AND IT IS THE EARLY END OF THE RANGE.** Lumentum mgmt: today's co-packaged optics is **"opportunistic rather than forced. Nvidia deploys it because a faster, more power efficient laser frees up power that can be redirected to the GPUs, which buys system performance. Copper has not run out of headroom yet."** And the date: **"She puts copper hitting the wall in late 2027. That is when the first scale-up CPO deployments arrive, or MPO fiber connectors where customers want a different configuration, driven by link lengths beyond roughly three meters connecting rack to rack."** On the six-metre copper claim: **"Credo and Broadcom argue six meters is achievable. On paper it is, but the power you would have to push through that copper makes it impractical, so the switch to optical happens around that point regardless"** (Lumentum mgmt via Aurelion Research, 2026-07-29). ⚠️ **This is a supplier's IR confirming late-'27 scale-up, and it sits against three marks already on this page: [../LITE.md](../LITE.md)'s own Director of Silicon-Photonics R&D (07-23: CPO trial ~2028, hyperscaler deployment ~2030, GPU-GPU copper for ≥1 more generation); MS/Marshall's 07-28 markdown of 2028 CPO SCALE ("not later, just smaller"); and the 650 Group / UBS / MS cluster at partial-2028 / meaningful-2029. Management's date is the most bullish of the set and the most direct in provenance. All stay; none is adopted.**
  - **THE POWER-BUDGET LEG — PHOTONCAP, AND IT IS THE BETTER MECHANISM.** "Agreed on the copper framing. **Copper's limit is not a date. It is a cost curve of reach, speed, and power**, and Kathy's answer to the six meter claims concedes it: doable, but the power bill kills it. The same logic cuts through 'voluntary.' **A data center runs against a fixed power envelope, so every watt saved on networking is a watt handed to the GPUs, and Nvidia's own material puts CPO at 3.5x lower power than pluggable transceivers. Copper is not forcing this wave; the power budget is. The scarcer power gets, the stronger this demand becomes. The weak spot is not the voluntariness but that it rides on one company's architecture choice**" (PhotonCap technical comment, 2026-07-29). **That last clause is the systemic risk for the whole complex and connects directly to the unresolved Fubon post-Kyber rack decision logged below.** Aurelion's own framing of the same point: **"voluntary demand is higher quality demand… You are buying two S-curves. The opportunistic one is largely played out. The scale-up one has yet to begin."**
  - **THE SUPPLY LEG — AND THIS IS THE NUMBER THAT CUTS AGAINST THE SCARCITY TRADE, VOLUNTEERED BY MANAGEMENT.** Asked about capacity: **"they are adding as fast as they can. Two fabs in Japan produce most of the world's supply of EML lasers, and capacity there is up eight-fold in two and a half years," offered as representative of adds made elsewhere. "Lumentum now runs five fabs, including a new one starting to ramp that is not yet producing wafers. Every site has had tools added, and several have had the building shell expanded to fit more."** ⚠️ **Aurelion flags the tension itself: "Eight-fold EML capacity in two and a half years cuts against a pure scarcity thesis. Lumentum is racing to relieve the bottleneck that gives it pricing power. We read that as confidence. Management is building for durable demand."** **PhotonCap converts it into the sharper and more useful claim — the best argument on this page against the shortage-premium trade as distinct from the demand thesis: "Wafer starts and qualified yield at the newest spec are different numbers. A fab-level capacity multiple does not relieve a 200G per lane shortage one to one, because the binding constraint is yield at the highest power bins, not floor space… Building capacity takes money. Hitting the top bin takes years of practice. That is why the eight fold number overstates the relief, and why the shortage premium itself is not durable while the margin that survives lives in yield and mix, not in the shortage price."** **Read the distinction precisely: it defends the earnings while attacking the reason the market currently pays for them — a multiple argument dressed as an engineering one, and the cleanest statement of it this page carries.** Against it, management's counter-marks: **the imbalance is still ~30% greater demand than is physically suppliable; all EML capacity is locked in LTAs through FY27; customers exceeding LTA coverage pay premium prices and non-signers "risk losing their entire allocation of supply"; double-digit 200G EML price increases are expected in FY26 on the absence of a second source; and a McKinsey study cited by Aurelion has 800G transceiver production falling short of demand by about HALF in FY27, with shortfalls extending through 2029.**
  - **THE MOAT, DEFINED PROPERLY — PHOTONCAP, AND THIS SHOULD BE THE PAGE'S WORKING DEFINITION.** Management's version is power ratings: **"competition thins out as power ratings climb. Producing 150 to 400 milliwatt lasers at high volume, high yield and high reliability is difficult,"** and she does not see many players able to do for Nvidia what Lumentum does today. PhotonCap sharpens it: the moat is **"not peak power. It is qualified usable power at operating temperature, multiplied by spectral quality, reliability, and high volume yield,"** made concrete in four terms — **RIN** (output flicker; under PAM4's four brightness levels too much flicker makes the receiver confuse levels), **COD** (catastrophic optical damage, where the laser's own light burns its output facet — "the wall you hit first as power climbs"), **SMSR** (spectral purity) and **FIT** (failures per billion device hours — "with millions of lasers in a data center, even a slightly high FIT means something dies every day"). The structural conclusion: **"Headline specs are what challengers close first. Distribution tails, noise, and lifetime statistics close last, because they take volume learning and years of field data rather than design. Lumentum's claimed edge sits exactly there, and for the same structural reason it cannot be externally verified yet."**
  - **THE EML-vs-CW PRODUCT MAP, AND WHY A CPO DELAY IS NOT A DEMAND EVENT.** PhotonCap: an **EML** integrates a DFB laser and an electro-absorption modulator on one InP die — a finished modulated source, and **"today's 800G and 1.6T pluggable transceivers hang off it. That is the line that is sold out right now."** A **CW laser** supplies unmodulated continuous light with the modulator on a silicon-photonics chip — **"that is the external light source for CPO and SiPh transceivers, and the high power line built for Nvidia sits here."** Net: **"EML is today's cash flow and high power CW is the next mix shift."** And the reframing of this page's biggest bear thread: **"If CPO slips on ASIC or packaging integration while bandwidth demand keeps growing, demand does not disappear. It stays in pluggables longer, and pluggables are EML intensive, which is the line that is sold out today. An integration driven delay pushes CW and ELSFP revenue out while extending the EML cycle. If the delay instead comes from a broader AI capex slowdown, both architectures get hurt."** Corollary: **the cannibalization question flips into a cushion in the delayed-CPO scenario — "CPO is one rung, and a delayed rung keeps traffic on the rung beneath it."** ⚠️ **"The June selloff punished the whole optical complex without this distinction" — a direct read on the June-10 de-rate already logged across [../LITE.md](../LITE.md) and [../COHR.md](../COHR.md).**
  - **A THIRD LIGHT-SOURCE PLATFORM, WHICH THIS PAGE DID NOT CARRY — GaAs VCSEL AT CONSUMER SCALE, NOW POINTED AT AI CHIP-TO-CHIP.** Management mentioned only "a VCSEL branch on the roadmap" (raised unprompted). PhotonCap: **"'A VCSEL branch on the roadmap' is weaker than the reality. Lumentum has manufactured GaAs VCSELs at consumer volume for smartphone 3D sensing for years, with more than 10 billion emitters shipped per company disclosure. At OFC 2026 it showed that base extended to AI: a 1060nm VCSEL and photodetector 2D monolithic array co-packaged with a host ASIC, targeting slow and wide scale-up protocols such as UCIe and PCIe, with operation above 150°C and channel sparing. So what sits on the roadmap is not VCSEL technology. It is commercial adoption in AI chip-to-chip links."** Because **VCSELs live on GaAs, not InP, Lumentum holds three light-source platforms at once — InP EML, InP high-power CW, and GaAs VCSEL — "a stronger architecture hedge than the paraphrase suggests."** ⚠️ **Directly relevant to this page's VCSEL thread: it cuts against Irrational Analysis's dismissal of 200G/400G VCSELs as reliability-doomed (05-08) by pointing at a slow-and-wide UCIe/PCIe use case rather than a high-speed serial one, and it puts a second named vendor behind the InP-91%→79% / VCSEL-9%→21% lane-share migration (LightCounting) alongside COHR's 200G GaAs VCSEL.**
  - **THE ARCHITECTURE-SCALING ARGUMENT — AURELION, AND IT IS THE RIGHT WAY TO FRAME THE THESIS.** "**There is a durable source of demand for optics based on the growth of compute domains, meaning the number of GPUs that must operate as one coherent unit. These domains are rapidly outgrowing what copper can serve. Nvidia has begun moving from 72 GPU domains toward as many as 576 by 2027, and copper is already confined on an NV72 rack. Once a domain encompasses multiple racks, linking must be optical regardless of copper's current efficiency. Thus, the optics thesis necessitates that AI systems continue scaling, not only that copper hits a wall or that 448G copper becomes unattainable. So, as CPO is anticipated to scale up in FY27 or FY28, it can do so in parallel with copper and still see commercial success.**" Plus a net-new copper-physics mechanism: **"Rubin relies on bi-directional signaling to reach its bandwidth targets, and bi-directional signaling over copper degrades substantially at 448G per lane,"** with unknown time-to-market for 448G copper, while **"optics can scale along modulation, fiber count, and wavelength multiplexing."** ⚠️ **Note this reframes what has to be true: the bull case no longer requires a copper failure, only continued domain growth — which is a materially lower bar than the "copper runs out" mechanism this page has often leaned on.** Aurelion is explicit that the common framing is "**slightly misguided**," citing Jensen on copper-for-scale-up / photonics-for-scale-out and **Broadcom saying optical scale-up "is not quite there yet."**
  - **THE CPO-UNIT CHANNEL NUMBERS, AND THE BEAR THEY WERE WRITTEN TO REBUT.** Aurelion's bull #4, sourced to **Global Semi Research's 2026-06-10 direct response to the SemiAnalysis note**: supply-chain checks put **NVIDIA's guidance to Lumentum and Coherent for high-power continuous-wave lasers at roughly 40MM units in January, rising to roughly 100MM by April and May**, which **back-solves to CPO switch shipments of roughly 200,000-300,000 in 2027, scaling toward 600,000-800,000 in 2028**, and **lines up with TSMC's COUPE plan.** ⚠️ **Aurelion's own weighting is retained: "These are channel figures rather than company disclosure, so we weight them accordingly."** The bear side, and the mechanism is net-new detail on this page: **SemiAnalysis's 2026-06-09 institutional report argued scale-out CPO shipments would be revised down through FY27 and scale-up pushed to FY29, "that CPO output is driven by a single ASIC that is very limited, and that ASIC integration is difficult with challenging underlying economics in its current state"** — the note behind the violent June selloff. **Set the two side by side: ~200-300k CPO switches in 2027 (channel) vs scale-up pushed to FY29 (SemiAnalysis) vs "smaller in 2028, not later" (MS 07-28). This page now holds three incompatible 2027-28 CPO sizings, and the honest position is that unit volume is not forecastable until NVDA's rack architecture is fixed.**
  - **SECOND SOURCING IS ALREADY HAPPENING — THE CLEANEST STATEMENT OF IT ON THIS PAGE.** PhotonCap: **"Concentration is a snapshot. Addressable demand is wider. Every camp pursuing CPO needs high power light. AMD's rack scale expansion creates EML, CW, and pluggable demand. Broadcom is a potential customer and at the same time a platform competitor with its own laser sources. Marvell can be a buyer, an ecosystem partner, and an integration layer competitor all at once. Absent a disclosed design win, though, these are demand paths, not confirmed customers. The reverse direction is also on the public record: second sourcing is in progress, not a hypothesis. NVIDIA's technical blog names three ELS vendors, Lumentum, Sumitomo, and Coherent. The same day Lumentum got its $2B, Coherent got an identical $2B. Coherent's OFC 2026 release formalizes its 6 inch InP ramp and a 400mW CW laser, and AAOI announced sampling of a 400mW narrow linewidth laser in December 2025. The time axis is what matters. Competing capacity lands outside the FY27 LTA window."** ⚠️ **The named ELS trio (Lumentum / Sumitomo / Coherent) is net-new — this page has carried Sumitomo only as an InP substrate/capacity name, never as a named NVDA external-light-source vendor. That is a direct nuance to the BTG 06-25 "essentially two, possibly three suppliers" framing: the third name is Sumitomo, and it is on NVDA's own list.** It also lines up with the WIN Semiconductors CW-laser project at **YE27 mass production** logged 07-26 — competing capacity clustering just outside FY27.
  - **CHINA — ZETTA NO LONGER FITS EITHER BOX, AND THAT IS THE UPDATE.** Management's position is that new Chinese entrants add capacity **"mostly in lower power lasers,"** with Lumentum's internal comparison showing materially higher yields in its own finished transceivers, better unit-to-unit spec uniformity and better reliability in test and field. ⚠️ **Aurelion's caveat is essential and they hold this view with the least conviction of their four: "The China comparison came from Lumentum's own lab… Nobody outside the company has checked it. And Chinese suppliers can close the gap over time. China has shown this so many times."** PhotonCap's update: **"Zetta no longer fits either box, not the low power dismissal and not the imminent catch-up call. On public record it announced a mass producible 100G PAM4 EML at OECC 2025 and followed with a 200G per lane device at ACP in November 2025. Device level performance deserves to be taken seriously now. The open question is… can it hold the full spec distribution across operating temperature and lifetime, and do unit-to-unit variation and noise behavior like RIN survive hyperscale qualification. That gets proven by years of volume data, not by announcements."** **Extends the DSBJ-200G-EML-MP-2H26 watch-item already on this page (Jefferies 07-14) with a second named Chinese vendor holding dated 200G/lane device announcements.**
  - **THE 4-INCH-vs-6-INCH LINE, WHICH IS THE COMPETITIVE ITEM.** Aurelion's bear #1: **"Coherent's competitive product is its 6 inch indium phosphide wafer line. By switching from 4 inch to 6 inch wafers, Coherent produces roughly 2.25 times the usable area per wafer, yielding more laser die at a lower cost per die. Lumentum still runs 4 inch wafers and is trying to catch up. If Coherent's 6 inch yield closes the gap on Lumentum's 4 inch performance, this deeply undermines Lumentum's pricing power,"** the bite being that **Lumentum framed its own advantage as bits per square area of InP, "so a competitor with more than twice the usable area per wafer makes direct headway on that metric."** Also: **"Coherent's indium phosphide lasers are also sold out through 2027."** ⚠️ **Two calibrations. First, 2.25x is a 4→6-inch AREA ratio and is NOT Citi's 4.36x chips/wafer (07-16), which is measured off a 3-inch base — both stand. Second, this sits against the 07-23 expert-call confirmation on [../LITE.md](../LITE.md) that Lumentum has bought a 6-inch III-V fab in North Carolina: current volume production 4-inch, Greensboro 6-inch. Stated, not reconciled.** On capacity timing, Aurelion has **Greensboro (ex-Qorvo, converting GaAs→InP as the fifth InP fab) contributing revenue "roughly six quarters out from the May call, which puts it in late 2027 at the earliest,"** plus **a separate new US facility funded alongside the Nvidia investment that is "a ground-up build rather than a conversion"** — with the bear that **"capacity commitments are being baked into current contracts at peak pricing against volumes that do not arrive for at least another 18 months"** and LITE **"expanding EML production by 40% annually."**
  - **WHERE THE VALUE SITS IN THE STACK — AURELION, AND IT IS A GOOD CROSS-CHECK ON THIS PAGE'S "OWN THE LIGHT SOURCE" THESIS.** "**Leading edge foundry capacity is arguably the most scarce layer in the entire supply chain, with TSMC being effectively the sole frontier supplier. HBM is also constrained across 3 global suppliers, and behaves as a commodity, with more capacity expected to come online within the next 18 to 24 months. Optical and photonic components are currently in a similar position to memory in terms of viable suppliers and industry barriers to entry, given the underlying material science in indium phosphide fabrication and reliability requirements.**" But the honest caveat, which this page should carry: **"training is the most communication intensive workload, and the way that optics have traditionally traded is around training economics. Inference is less network intensive, or at least has been historically, so a mix shift toward inference does not translate to a 1:1 demand for optics"** — closing over time via longer context windows, heavier routing, disaggregated prefill/decode and agentic usage, plus geographically distributed inference driving DCI. PhotonCap's live example: **the AMD–Cerebras July joint inference solution, where "Helios handles the front half, reading the prompt, and Cerebras handles the back half, generating the answer, so work in progress crosses a network between two machines" — with the bound kept: "What that link is made of has not been disclosed. So this is evidence that inference moves a lot of data, not evidence of any vendor's revenue."** Net: **"Optics at large are insulated from model commoditization, because they benefit from bits moved rather than tokens generated across infrastructure."** [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/A_Conversation_with_Lumentum.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-28) — 🔴 THE NPO/CPO QUESTION NOW SITS INSIDE [[NVDA]]'s UNRESOLVED POST-KYBER RACK DECISION — AND ON THE SAME DAY MS's OPTICAL ANALYST CUTS HER VIEW OF 2028 CPO SCALE. TWO SOURCES, PULLING OPPOSITE WAYS, BOTH LOGGED (Fubon "Our advanced packaging, CoWoS forecast adjustments; other updates" · Sherman Shang / Daniel Yang, 2026-07-28; Morgan Stanley webcast "2Q26 memory, Intel implications and ARM preview" · Meta Marshall, 2026-07-28).**
  - **THE FUBON LEG — WHERE THE OPTICS DECISION ACTUALLY LIVES.** Following **NVDA's cancellation/delay of the Kyber rack architecture in March/April "due to mid-plane bandwidth issues,"** Fubon says NVDA is working on replacements that hold the compute-die count constant. **Kyber was 576 compute dies in the rack — 144 GPUs × 4 compute dies each — and "our checks suggest nVidia still plans to put the same amount of compute dies in its new rack architecture or system."** **Two candidates, neither finalised.** **(a) The conservative one: keep the current Oberon rack, and build a system from FOUR Oberon racks connected by traditional scale-out** — each Oberon rack carries 144 compute dies, so 4 × 144 = 576. **(b) A two-rack design Fubon calls "Taycan": each rack has 144 GPUs with TWO compute dies per GPU, and "between the two racks, nVidia plans to use NPO/CPO for scale out connection while inside the rack, cable connection is still used for scale up connection."** **Read the structure, because it is the whole point for this page: option (a) is a copper/traditional-scale-out outcome with no incremental optics content; option (b) puts NPO/CPO on the critical path of NVDA's next flagship system. The two candidates are not neutral for the optical complex — they are the bull and the bear.** Fubon's own posture: **"At the moment, the rack design for both solutions is not yet finalized. Therefore, it is difficult to say who will benefit and who will suffer. But we think nVidia is still aggressive in the NPO/CPO solution despite a lot of market noise."** ⚠️ **Note what that sentence does and does not do — it is a read on NVDA's INTENT, explicitly withheld from naming winners and losers. Do not convert it into a supplier call.** (Fubon 2026-07-28) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Our_advanced_packaging_CoWoS_forecast_adjustments_other_up.html)
  - **THE MORGAN STANLEY LEG — AND IT CUTS THE OTHER WAY.** On the same day, MS's optical analyst **Meta Marshall** describes the buy-side optical bias as **"maybe a little bit more cautious, just because of concerns around CPO timing."** The precise formulation matters and is unusually careful: **"I think we don't lean on there being delays in terms of what we've seen [with] CPO, but just the amount of scale that we think that it will have is just a little bit lower in 2028 versus kind of where expectations had been. That's created a little bit of a breather on the space in general."** **So: not later, just smaller. That is a distinct claim from every delay call already on this page, and it is the more damaging one for 2028 numbers, because a delay pushes revenue right while a scale cut removes it.** Where she does see interest: **networking, "particularly Arista, where kind of expectations are that the [product cycle] could see acceleration next year"** — attractive precisely because most comparable names are decelerating by then. (Morgan Stanley webcast · Meta Marshall, 2026-07-28)
  - **⚠️ THE TENSION, FLAGGED AND DELIBERATELY NOT RESOLVED. Fubon says NVDA is leaning INTO NPO/CPO ("still aggressive… despite a lot of market noise") at exactly the moment MS marks DOWN the scale CPO will reach in 2028.** They are not strictly contradictory — **NVDA can be committed to the architecture while the 2028 unit volume still lands below where the sell-side had it, especially if the four-Oberon option wins for the first system and CPO arrives on the generation after.** But they point opposite directions for anyone sizing 2028 optical content, and **the Fubon rack decision is plausibly the very thing that resolves the MS scale question.** Both stay on the page. **The dated, checkable item is NVDA's rack-architecture finalisation** — until that lands, 2028 CPO scale is not a forecastable number.
  - **MARSHALL'S CORNING READ, WHICH IS THE SUPPLY-SIDE COUNTERWEIGHT TO HER OWN CAUTION.** On the [../GLW.md](../GLW.md) print that morning: **optical results "generally within expectations," with the enterprise business RE-ACCELERATING to +65% y/y growth but telco "essentially flat"** — a surprise she flags as possibly supply reallocation. **The guide "did not show a tremendous amount of upside"**, light on both revenue and EPS versus MS's own bogey (**"we were forecasting about 200 million of upside that they could have posted on the revenue side"** and the couple of cents on EPS). **Her interpretation is the interesting part: she reads the light guide as evidence of SOLD-OUT CONDITIONS in optical rather than demand weakness** — *"just kind of showing the sold-out conditions within optical."* And on the multiple: valuations **"had definitely rerated from historical levels… really what we're doing now is kind of contracting more towards historical valuations, but still on much more elevated EPS."** **Read for the theme: the optical de-rating in progress is a multiple event on a rising earnings base, not an earnings event — which is a materially different setup from the 2028-scale worry above and should not be conflated with it.** (Morgan Stanley webcast · Meta Marshall, 2026-07-28) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_28_2q26_ms_memory_intel_implications_and_arm_preview.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — CPO shows up in the industry's biggest packaging debate as a REASON TO PICK TSMC, and nothing else — worth logging precisely because of how little it contains (UBS · Sunny Lin / Randy Abrams / Nicolas Gaudois / Timothy Arcuri / Shingo Hirata / Jerry Su / Diana Chang "Can Intel challenge TSMC's advanced packaging leadership? — Intel's EMIB-T vs TSMC's CoPoS", 2026-07-27).** ⚠️ **Read the honest framing first: this 18-page note on the next generation of AI packaging contains NO CPO unit forecast, no CPO revenue, no port counts and no CPO date. CPO appears exclusively as a forward roadmap attribute of TSMC's CoPoS platform — an argument for why customers should stay with TSMC, not evidence of near-term optical volume.** That is itself the datapoint: **on the two platforms that will carry AI accelerators in 2028-30, CPO is a differentiator on the slide and not yet a line in the model.** **What UBS actually says, verbatim.** CoPoS **"should offer stronger package-level performance through higher interconnect density, better signal/power integrity, richer integration options such as embedded capacitors, 3D stacking and eventually co-packaged optics (CPO)"** — note *eventually*. And on the competitive comparison: **"CoPoS could achieve denser, shorter interconnects, and a higher level of integration, including 3D stacking, more embedded silicon capacitors, and integration of co-packaged optics (CPO)."** In UBS's head-to-head table (Figure 4/12) the **"Ecosystem Strength"** row reads **EMIB-T: "Growing support from hyperscalers seeking alternative supply" vs CoPoS: "Deep ecosystem spanning foundry, CoWoS, SoIC and future CPO" — verdict "CoPoS."** And the summary of TSMC's moat: **"TSMC's edge is its strong capability to provide end-to-end solution, combining front-end process with a comprehensive technology portfolio of 2.5D/3D packaging and CPO."** **The structural read for this page: TSMC is using future CPO integration as a retention argument against a cost-and-scale challenger TODAY, before CPO carries meaningful volume — which is exactly what you would expect if CPO's economic value to TSMC is captured through platform lock-in rather than through CPO revenue itself.**
  - **The one dated, actionable CPO item — and it is a scenario, not a forecast: "TSMC's share could further increase if Google's new TPUs (eg. v10) return to CoPoS and adopt CPO integration."** So on UBS's own map, **the v10 generation is the first named accelerator program where CPO adoption is a live commercial variable**, and it is framed as a *reversion* — v9 leaving TSMC for Intel EMIB-T, v10 potentially coming back **because** of CPO. **That makes CPO integration a competitive weapon in a packaging share fight, which is a demand mechanism this page has not previously carried.** The v9/v10 packaging leg sits on [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md); ⚠️ note this cuts against the Jefferies 06-29 read already in Sources that **TPU v9's 400G SerDes scale-up interconnect is "co-packaged copper," not optics** — v9 copper, v10 the CPO question. Not contradictory, but the timeline is now explicit.
  - **The timing this implies, and it is consistent with the marks already on this page.** CoPoS itself reaches **mass production in 2028 (Nvidia's Feynman likely first, H2'28-2029), with AMD and ASICs following in 2029-30**, on **310×310mm² panels migrating to 510×515mm²**; TSMC **installed the CoPoS pilot line in late Q226** and will fix the **first-generation process flow, equipment specifications and key suppliers by mid-2027.** CPO-on-CoPoS therefore cannot be a volume event before the platform itself ramps — i.e. **2029+ at the earliest for the ASIC programs**, which corroborates UBS's own 07-02 **GPU-CPO ~2029E** mark and sits alongside MS's "material adoption no earlier than 2029" and 650 Group's "partial 2028, meaningful 2029." **Nothing here pulls CPO forward.** The mid-2027 supplier-selection window is the one date optical-equipment holders should have in the diary.
  - **A glass read that matters to the CPO thread on this page, and it is SLOWER than the marks logged 07-26.** UBS separates **panel carriers** (temporary platform for RDL-interposer formation in the chip-last CoP flow — "glass is the leading candidate based on current visibility") from **glass-core substrates** (next-gen ABF with a glass core and high-aspect-ratio TGVs). Its call: **"glass adoption (whether for panel carrier or glass-core substrate) should not be a major yield or production risk for the ramp of either TSMC's CoPoS or Intel's EMIB-T in 2028… both packaging technologies should be in mass production in 2028 without requiring glass-core substrates,"** with glass-core MP **"unlikely to reach mass production before 2029-30E, if not later"** (Ibiden's own slide: **2030+**). ⚠️ **Slower than MS's per-player ramps (Innolux+TSMC+Ibiden MP 2H28-2029; Lens Tech+Intel meaningful volume 2H27) — carried as a competing view, not a correction. For the optical read specifically it cuts a modest negative on the "glass substrate as CPO-era enabler" thesis attached to [../GLW.md](../GLW.md) (BofA 07-06 framed glass substrates in 2029-30 as CPO enablers — UBS's timing agrees with that, and is later than the 2H28 bulls).** Full packaging detail, the EMIB-T-vs-CoPoS architecture comparison and the CoPoS supplier map (incl. ASE/SPIL on-substrate, All Ring underfill, GPTC wet process, Chroma metrology) on [cowos-packaging.md](cowos-packaging.md). [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/UBS_on_adv_packaging.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — the best-quantified AI optical order datapoint available, and it is a ~2x beat against a bogey investors were debating the day before (JPM · Scott Silver, "Silver Linings Euro TMT Weekly," 2026-07-26):** on Nokia's 2Q26 print — **"The standout for me was the AI order intake, with €2.8bn materially ahead of the €1.3-1.5bn buy-side bogey that many investors were debating yesterday, and a level that far exceeded even the more bullish expectations."** Two reasons this is more useful than a typical peer order print: the bogey was **known and actively debated 24 hours earlier**, so it is a clean surprise measurement rather than a retrospective; and **Nokia is a systems vendor buying the optics**, so its AI order intake is upstream demand for exactly the transceiver/laser content [[LITE]] and [[COHR]] sell. ⚠️ **The market reaction is the part to internalise, not the beat: the stock "revers[ed] from an opening gain of around 7% and roughly flat post the call… despite what I view as an exceptionally strong set of numbers," after already falling ~40% from its highs** — with Silver adding "I struggle to identify what more the company realistically could have delivered today beyond raising guidance." Same week's pattern at INTC (-8% on a beat that "cleared expectations by wide margins"), TSM, ASML, GOOGL and GEV. Feedback has Nokia sustaining a **20-25x multiple → €12-15/share on 2028 numbers**; JPM (Sandeep Deshpande) OW; short interest just **1 ADTV / 1% of free float**. Optical multiples derated **-15% since the June 22 SOX peak**, having peaked earlier than the rest of the complex (Jefferies).
- **NEW (2026-07-26) — merchant compound-semi capacity is being added into the laser bottleneck, with a dated CW-laser entry at YE27 (JPM · Jerry Tsai, WIN Semiconductors, OW, PT NT$336 → NT$373, 2026-07-26):** JPM raised WIN's **2026 revenue growth to 30% from ~10% prior** (Infra +40%+, **Optical +50%+**) and 2027 from 13% to 20%, driven by **earlier-than-expected optical receiver chip (PD) mass production — "late 2Q vs 2H26"** — plus LEO; utilisation **65% (2Q) → 70%+ (3Q26e)**; capex reiterated at **NT$2-3bn, "significantly higher than the annual capex in prior years."** **Two details matter for this theme.** The demand concentration: **WIN has only ONE PD project in mass production, yet "the order scale of this customer (likely a major US design house) alone is capable of driving AI-related optical comm to reach a high-single-digit mix of total revenue in 2H26e, which has more than doubled vs 2025,"** rising to a double-digit mix in 2027e. And the competitive one: alongside **short-distance VCSEL and long-distance EML laser chips already in mass production since 2025**, WIN has **"the CW laser project with a major US optical chip IDM (mass production should take place in YE27)."** That is the first dated timeline on this page for **third-party merchant capacity entering the CW/ultra-high-power laser bottleneck** that BTG's LightCounting expert (06-25) characterised as effectively a two-supplier moat. **Not a 2026 threat — a 2028 one — but it is the supply response the moat thesis has to survive.** WIN's own shares are **-38% over three months vs TaiEx +12%**, which JPM attributes partly to "weak sentiment on both LEO and optical communications."
- **NEW (2026-07-25) — the cleanest structural argument yet for why the InP squeeze outlasts the DRAM one, plus an unverified claim that Lumentum has rationed to a single customer (Irrational Analysis, "Stranded Indium-Phosphide", 2026-07-25).** An engineering-driven Substack; the author self-discloses being **long Lumentum, AXTI and AAOI** — bias noted. **(1) The framing, from an industry contact he endorses: "Only 25% of the world's DRAM goes to AI. Can just take more from consumer. 80% of InP already going to AI."** He says to ignore the precise percentages but insists the core argument holds — **InP has essentially zero consumer end-market, so unlike DRAM there is no non-AI allocation to raid.** In memory you can "metaphorically shoot the smartphone industry and annex their DRAM allocation"; in InP that release valve does not exist. This is the mechanism behind Citi's 07-16 "InP Supply Constraint Will Likely Persist" model, stated qualitatively. **(2) The allocation claim, unverified but specific: "Lumentum cut everyone except Nvidia off. Ayar did not remove Lumentum from their website. Lumentum removed Ayar."** If true, [../LITE.md](../LITE.md) is trading scarce InP capacity for NVDA exclusivity, and the Seivers→Ayar Labs→AMD chain that FinTwit has been trading is dead. Single-source, author is long LITE — **log as a claim to confirm, not a fact.** **(3) A new InP-consuming architecture, modelled: SOA-based ELSFP.** For an 8-fiber, 1310nm, 22 dBm ELSFP aimed at NPO/CPO, he compares the conventional **8× discrete DFB** build against a **high-power SOA** design. The SOA route has **better InP area efficiency** (more photons per unit of InP) — which matters in a shortage — but at product level it still needs a **CW seed laser** and loses heavily to **coupling losses** from extra lenses/isolators and beam-shape mismatch. Result: **51% gross margin the normal way vs 17% the SOA way.** "The math works. Kinda hilarious to be honest." **(4) Who can actually build it:** "the usual suspects (**Lumentum, Coherent, Broadcom, Sumitomo, Furukawa, AAOI**) do not know how to make this niche device because they have been too busy making money in real markets" — only ex-LiDAR firms have high-power SOA capability, and he names just two as viable: **one unnamed private IDM, and $AEVA**, which manufactures with **Seivers** as its InP fab partner. He is separately scathing on Seivers' DWDM monolithic array ("the yield is horrific") while conceding Seivers may make more money from Aeva's SOA than it ever did from Ayar Labs. **Net for the theme: an InP-efficiency workaround to the shortage exists, it is real engineering, and it is uneconomic at ~17% GM — so the shortage does not get designed around cheaply.** Cross-ref [../AXTI.md](../AXTI.md), [../LITE.md](../LITE.md), [../COHR.md](../COHR.md), [../AAOI.md](../AAOI.md).
- **NEW (2026-07-25) — supply-side watch: a GaAs and InP substrate project was approved in Zhejiang province, China** (TrendForce, 2026-07-23, via Susquehanna · Mehdi Hosseini's Technology Hardware weekly, 2026-07-25). Feeds the open question on this page of how fast China's domestic InP substrate chain verticalizes behind its EML entrants — relevant to the AXT + Sumitomo ~80% ex-China share. Also from the same weekly: **Aixtron's Q1'26 order intake was €171m (+30% y/y) with optoelectronics at 65% of equipment orders**, and UBS forecasts **Aixtron power sales -28% y/y in FY26** — i.e. the MOCVD tool cycle is now almost entirely an optical story (see [../AIXA.md](../AIXA.md)).
- **NEW (2026-07-24) — the MI500 optical roadmap is now specified, and it is the most concrete scale-up-optics datapoint on this page: 9 optical engines per package at 6.4 Tbit/s uni-di each = 57.6T of scale-up bandwidth, 32 lanes of 200G per engine, each lane 8 lambdas (4 Tx / 4 Rx) at 50G NRZ, light from ELSFPs also at 6.4T each (SemiAnalysis "Can AMD break the CUDA Moat? AMD Advancing AI 2026", 2026-07-24).** This resolves the open question the Jefferies 07-16 bullet below flagged into AMD's event ("whether AMD commits to a CPO-based scale-up solution and who supplies the optical engine") with actual architecture. **"On its next generation of chips, the MI500s series, AMD should be pivoting to co-packaged copper (CPC) and near-packaged optics (NPO)."** The specification: **9 optical engines of 6.4 Tbit/s uni-di each = 57.6T of scale-up bandwidth per package**; **each engine supports 32 lanes of 200 Gbit/s**, and **each lane is composed of 8 lambdas — 4 transmit and 4 receive — at 50G NRZ modulation**; **the light source comes from ELSFPs that also support 6.4 Tbit/s uni-di each** (i.e. an **external-laser architecture**, which is the single most important read for the laser/EML vendors on this page — the light source stays pluggable and separately sourced rather than being integrated). **Topology:** the scale-up network *"should remain simple and based on a flat, one-tier network, connecting **576 GPUs across 8 racks**,"* preserving one-hop GPU-to-GPU connectivity to limit tail latency, on **288 switches** built on a **Tomahawk 6 SKU supporting 115.2T** (versus the 102.4T variant used in Helios), each switch connecting to the 576 GPUs at 200G per lane so multiple paths exist to any GPU. **Why optics is forced in — the reach/bandwidth wall, stated plainly:** Helios today is all copper (**10,368 differential pairs of copper cable per rack**, 72 lanes of 200G UALoE per GPU = 1.8 TB/s uni-di scale-up through 12 Broadcom Tomahawk 6 102.4T switches), and *"**MI500 is expected to extend the scale-up domain to 256 GPUs across three racks, where copper will likely give way to co-packaged copper or co-packaged optics**"* — note the source gives both a **256-GPU/3-rack** framing for the domain extension and the **576-GPU/8-rack** flat topology above; carry both as stated rather than reconciling them. Read for the theme: **the scale-up domain crossing a single rack is the trigger event for optics**, and AMD's chosen answer is **NPO + external light source, not full CPO** — consistent with the "NPO is the next best thing and captures lots of dollars" thread already on this page. (SemiAnalysis "Can AMD break the CUDA Moat? AMD Advancing AI 2026", 2026-07-24; see [../AMD.md](../AMD.md), [../AVGO.md](../AVGO.md), [../LITE.md](../LITE.md), [../COHR.md](../COHR.md), [../ALAB.md](../ALAB.md); the package/CoWoS leg is on [cowos-packaging.md](cowos-packaging.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_24_ENC_Can_AMD_break_the_CUDA_Moat_AMD_Advancing_AI_2026_email.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-09) — a long-reach-optics demand driver the router missed entirely: Meta's scale-across architecture stitches 27 datacenters across 6 campuses into ~22 petabits/s, and the L3↔L4 hop is explicitly "a mix of LR optics and DWDM systems with ZR optics" (SemiAnalysis "The Future of Meta Superintelligence: A 1 Year Progress Update", 2026-07-09).** This page already carries SemiAnalysis's *sizing* of scale-across content (~$3B in 2026 → ~$14B in 2028, split ZR/ZR+ pluggables and Optical Line Systems); this is the **architectural mechanism** behind it, from a named deployment. **Prometheus is "a constellation of 27 datacenters spread across 6 campuses — 5 of these are within 6km of each other and the 6th is 75 to 80km away from the rest."** It is stitched by **AI-Backbone (AIBB)**: multiple **L3 Superspines (Backend Aggregation, "BAG")** each interconnecting **up to 5 DSF or 7 NSF scale-out regions** (mixable), aggregated onto **a single L4 Inter-BAG hub providing around 22 petabits per second of bi-directional bandwidth across the whole Prometheus cluster**. The layer placement is what generates the optical bill: **DSF/NSF regions fit inside a single datacenter room; the L3 layer sits within a single campus; the L4 primarily interconnects campuses** — and **"the connection between L3 and L4 is a mix of LR optics and Dense Wave Division Multiplexing (DWDM) systems with ZR optics, depending on the length of the fiber to reach the other campus."** **The physics that forces the architecture:** latency inside a DSF/NSF region is **1-10µs**, but **"the latency to reach a site that is 100km apart cannot be lower than 500µs, just due to light propagation in the fiber,"** which forces asynchronous training strategies — **pretraining synchronous in one region, RL spread globally**. And the forward datapoint: **"the other Titans will go even further with scale-across, connecting campuses up to 2,000kms apart."** ⚠️ **Flagged as a READ-THROUGH, not a company datapoint: campus-to-campus scale-across at 22 Pbit/s and 2,000km implies materially more coherent DWDM/ZR and long-reach optics content per GW than a single-campus design — but the source names no optical vendor and gives no port counts, so no supplier attribution can be drawn from it.** (SemiAnalysis "The Future of Meta Superintelligence: A 1 Year Progress Update", 2026-07-09; see [../META.md](../META.md); the DC/power leg is on [ai-datacenter-power.md](ai-datacenter-power.md), the vendor sizing already on this page under the SemiAnalysis scale-across thread) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_09_ENC_The_Future_of_Meta_Superintelligence_A_1_Year_Progress_Update_email.html)
- **NEW (2026-06-14, one line) — evidence CPO optical engines are already shipping in commercial volume: SemiAnalysis's teardown lab has reverse-engineered "a major TSMC customer's COUPE CPO optical engine + EIC 3D stack" (SemiAnalysis / STEEL Team, "Is SMIC N+3's Metal Pitch Smaller than Intel 18A's?", 2026-06-14).** In describing its Oregon teardown lab, SemiAnalysis notes it has "already generated revenue on advanced datacenter chip teardowns including our recent **reverse engineering of a major TSMC customer's COUPE CPO optical engine + EIC 3D stack**." A small but useful existence proof for this page's central timing debate: **COUPE-based CPO engines are shipping in enough volume for a commercial teardown to be worth funding** — i.e. the technology is past the demo stage even where scale-up CPO deployment timing remains contested (see the "scale-up CPO is further out than modeled" thread below). The customer is not named. (SemiAnalysis / STEEL Team, 2026-06-14; see [../TSM.md](../TSM.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_06_14_ENC_Is_SMIC_N3s_Metal_Pitch_Smaller_than_Intel_18As_email.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-23) — Lumentum SiPh-R&D expert call: EMLs "revert to the major share" at 3.2T (mid/late-'27 → '28); scale-up optics takes off "late calendar '27"; every optical line is capacity-short now (LITE expert call — Director of Silicon-Photonics R&D, 2026-07-23).** A Lumentum silicon-photonics R&D leader framed the near-term AI-optical stack as **CPO optical modules + transceivers + externally-modulated lasers (EMLs)**, with LITE supplying **the lasers into NVIDIA's CPO scale-up partnership**. Two forward nuances for the EML-vs-CW debate this page tracks: (i) **at 3.2T, EMLs "are going to revert back to taking the major share"** (timing ~mid-to-late 2027 or 2028) after the CW-laser-for-CPO interim; (ii) **scale-up optics "really takes off late calendar '27"** (aligned to Jensen's comment that the ~late-'27 rack payment structure goes optical). The gating caveat: the whole market is **so capacity-short right now that even higher EML demand couldn't be supplied anyway** — reinforcing the supply-gated up-cycle framing. (LITE expert call, 2026-07-23; see [../LITE.md](../LITE.md), [../COHR.md](../COHR.md), [../NVDA.md](../NVDA.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_23_lite_call_23_jul_26.html)
- **NEW (2026-07-16) — JPM IT Hardware/Optics preview: the optical content is DCI + pluggable/coherent optics, NOT co-packaged optics — no CPO timing/port counts given (J.P. Morgan "IT Hardware/Optics preview", 2026-07-16).** _IMPORTANT label caveat:_ the note's optical content is **DCI + pluggable/coherent optics** ("optical transceivers," "coherent plugs," "scale across") — **not CPO** — with no CPO timing or port counts provided. [../CSCO.md](../CSCO.md) gets a **DCI tailwind via optical transceivers** plus an emerging bundled routing/coherent-plug "scale-across" portfolio for 2027. The note flags "material upside" to optical-transceiver supply from a **transcription-garbled vendor name (phonetically closest to [../CRDO.md](../CRDO.md) Credo — UNVERIFIED)**; recorded here as an **unverified transceiver-supplier reference, NOT firmly attributed to any ticker.** Names explicitly ABSENT from the note (scanned): [../LITE.md](../LITE.md), [../ANET.md](../ANET.md), [../MRVL.md](../MRVL.md), [../CIEN.md](../CIEN.md), [../AVGO.md](../AVGO.md), [../ALAB.md](../ALAB.md).
